\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists_{F(\bar{\pi})}}

By assumption
\psi\in\mathbb{L}_{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}}(\Gamma^{\prime})
can be lifted. Hence, there is a formula
\Psi_{0}\in\mathbb{L}_{\lambda}^{\mathcal{M}}(\bar{\Gamma}^{\prime})
such that
g^{*}(F\Psi_{0})=\Psi
. Using the right hand square above, one can see that
\exists_{\bar{\pi}}\Psi_{0}
is a lift for
\Phi
.

This shows that the map is surjective.
∎

As an immediate consequence of
lemma

4.15
, we can establish the
4^{th}
invariance theorem in the special case where
F:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
is a Barton trivial fibration. We will use this result to be able to establish
4^{th}
invariance theorem for the general case later on.

Theorem 4.16
.

Let
F:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
be a Barton trivial fibration between weak model categories. Then for any cofibrant
\Gamma\in\mathcal{M}
the induced map
h\mathbb{L}F_{A}:h\mathbb{L}_{\lambda}^{\mathcal{M}}(\Gamma)\to h\mathbb{L}_{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}}(F\Gamma)
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

By the previous
lemma

4.8
we know that
h\mathbb{L}F_{\Gamma}:h\mathbb{L}_{\lambda}^{\mathcal{M}}(\Gamma)\to h\mathbb{L}_{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}}(F\Gamma)
is injective. Next we can use
lemma

4.15
by observing that this surjectivity also descends to the level of
h\mathbb{L}F_{\Gamma}:h\mathbb{L}_{\lambda}^{\mathcal{M}}(\Gamma)\to h\mathbb{L}_{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}}(F\Gamma)
.
∎

Since our next goal is to prove
4^{th}
invariance theorem, with
theorem

4.16
at hand, we simply need to reduce our problem to the case in which we have Barton trivial fibrations. The constructions to come are essentially the necessary steps for this reduction process.

4.3
Path objects for weak model categories

The next step is to build some sort of “path object” for (weak) model category so that we can emulate Brown Factorization lemma to factor a general Quillen equivalence into a retract of a Barton trivial fibration followed by a Barton fibration. Ideally, we would want for a model category
\mathcal{M}
, we would like to build a diagram of left Quillen functors

\mathcal{M}\to P\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}

where the maps
P\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
are Barton trivial fibrations, and then try to use it to follow the proof of Brown’s Factorization Lemma. Unfortunately, that is not going to be quite possible: we will not be able to construct a map
\mathcal{M}\to P\mathcal{M}
. Instead, similarly to the proof of the
3^{rd}
invariance theorem, we will construct, a diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

where the arrow
p
is a Barton trivial fibration. This will turn out to be sufficient to build our desired Brown style factorization. The weak model categories
R\mathcal{M}
and
P\mathcal{M}
will be constructed as certain category of functors
\mathcal{M}^{J}
and
\mathcal{M}^{I}
, equipped with certain localization of the Reedy model structure. So we get a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{I}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the arrow on the left and the two maps
\mathcal{M}^{I}\to\mathcal{M}
induced by the projections are Barton trivial fibrations. More precisely, the construction we do takes as input a left Quillen equivalence
F:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
between weak model categories and produces a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{N}_{F}^{I}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{N}\times\mathcal{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where again the arrow on the left and the two maps
\mathcal{N}_{F}^{I}\to\mathcal{N}
induced by the projections are Barton trivial fibrations. Hence, the first diagram is a particular case when
F=Id_{\mathcal{M}}
.

Remark 4.17
.

The core idea of the outline above is already present in the proof of the
3^{rd}
invariance theorem. This can be seen as the analogue (or rather a dual) of the diagram (
1
) that appears in the proof of the
3^{rd}
invariance theorem, and it will play the exact same role. In both cases, the idea is to obtain some sort of Brown factorization.

The bulk of the work lies in endowing the categories
\mathcal{M}^{I}
and
\mathcal{M}^{J}
with the correct weak model structures. This can be summarized as follows: We start with the Reedy weak model structure on the category
\mathcal{M}^{J}
, or
\mathcal{N}^{I}
, and perform a “right Bousfield localization” to obtain our desired models.

Remark 4.18
.

The weak model structure on
\mathcal{N}^{I}
encodes a pair of objects
A,B
in
\mathcal{N}
with a “correspondence” between them; that is, a homotopy equivalence encoded by a cofibration
A\coprod B\to C
where both maps
A\to C
and
B\to C
are trivial cofibrations. The weak model structure we obtain on
\mathcal{M}^{J}
encodes objects
X
in
\mathcal{M}
equipped with a (weak) cylinder object, so that we can send such an object
X
with a cylinder
IX
to the correspondence
X\coprod X\to IX
.

4.3.1
Weak model for objects with weak cylinders

We start by fixing a weak model category
\mathcal{M}
and let
J
be the category

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

such that
ki=kj
. Consider the degree function making
J
into a direct category,
\deg(a)=0,\;\deg(b)=1,\;\deg(c)=2
. Our first goal is to prove:

Theorem 4.19
.

The category of diagrams
\mathcal{M}^{J}
has a weak model structure where:

1.

A map between diagrams
X\to Y
is a cofibration if

(a)

It is a Reedy cofibration,

(b)

Y_{a}\sqcup_{X_{a}}X_{c}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Y_{c}
and
Y_{b}\sqcup_{X_{b}}X_{c}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Y_{c}
are trivial cofibrations in
\mathcal{M}
.

2.

Fibrations are level-wise fibrations.

Remark 4.20
.

The theorem above make reference to Reedy cofibrations, therefore we must justify first that
\mathcal{M}^{J}
carries the Reedy weak model structure. Fortunately, this has been addressed in
theorem

C.11
.

Notation 4.21
.

For the sake of clarity, we denote by
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Reedy}
when referring to the Reedy weak model structure and
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
for the weak model structure of
theorem

4.19
. Of course,
a priori,
we have yet to prove that the latter is indeed a weak model structure. Therefore, whenever we say, for example, that a map
f:X\to Y
is a cofibration we just mean that
f
satisfies the corresponding condition of
theorem

4.19
.

We will justify that the following construction, which is simply the conditions of the theorem, is the correct one.

Observation 4.22
.

One can verify that in this new model structure, the core fibrations and core trivial cofibrations coincide with the ones in the Reedy weak model structure (see
lemma

4.25
).

The reader might suspect that this is not a fortuitous coincidence, these suspicions are well justified. As we mentioned, what we have done is a right Bousfield localization of a Reedy weak model structure on
\mathcal{M}^{J}
. Such localizations are studied in
[
Hen23
]
in the case when
\mathcal{M}
is a combinatorial (accessible) weak model category. Due to the lack of a general theorem that justifies the existence of these localizations producing a weak model category, we verify all required conditions by hand.

We examine the class of cofibrations. For a diagram
X\in\mathcal{M}^{J}
, the latching objects are
L_{a}X=\emptyset
,
L_{b}X=X_{a}\sqcup X_{a}
and
L_{c}X=X_{b}\sqcup_{X_{a}}X_{b}
. These are cofibrant in
\mathcal{M}
. Then a map
f:X\to Y
being a cofibration means that
X_{a}\hookrightarrow Y_{a}
,

X_{b}\sqcup_{X_{a}\sqcup X_{a}}(Y_{a}\sqcup Y_{a})\hookrightarrow Y_{b}\text{ and }X_{c}\sqcup_{(X_{b}\sqcup_{X_{a}}X_{b})}(Y_{b}\sqcup_{Y_{a}}Y_{b})\hookrightarrow Y_{c}

are cofibrations in
\mathcal{M}
, and additionally
Y_{a}\sqcup_{X_{a}}X_{c}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Y_{c}
and
Y_{b}\sqcup_{X_{b}}X_{c}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Y_{c}
are trivial cofibrations in
\mathcal{M}
.

Therefore, a diagram
Y\in\mathcal{M}^{J}
is
cofibrant
if
Y_{a}
is a cofibrant object in
\mathcal{M}
,

Y_{a}\sqcup Y_{a}\hookrightarrow Y_{b}\text{ and }Y_{b}\sqcup_{Y_{a}}Y_{b}\hookrightarrow Y_{c}

are cofibrations, and additionally
Y_{a}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Y_{c}
and
Y_{b}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Y_{c}
are trivial cofibrations. Spelling out the second Reedy condition gives us the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{a}\sqcup Y_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

This says that both maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Yi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Yj}
are cofibrations. We can use this on the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}\sqcup_{Y_{a}}Y_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

to conclude that
Y_{b}\hookrightarrow Y_{c}
is a cofibration. Of course this is in principle not necessary since we also have
Y_{b}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Y_{c}
is a trivial cofibration, but the novel aspect is that this follows only from Reedy cofibrancy. We also have a trivial cofibration
Y_{a}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Y_{c}
, by the two-out-of-three property the maps
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Yi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Yj}
are trivial cofibrations. We collect the above in the following:

Remark 4.23
.

If
Y
is cofibrant then we obtain the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{a}\sqcup Y_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{c}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\triangledown}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

This is just to say that cofibrant diagrams of
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
encode objects of
\mathcal{M}
for which a weak cylinder exists in the sense of
C.6
.

We reiterate that our goal is to show that the category of diagrams
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
has a weak model structure on it, where the cofibrations are the ones as specified in
theorem

4.19
. We begin by showing the following lemmas which are expected results in the theory of right Bousfield localizations.

Lemma 4.24
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
cofibrant. Then, a map
X\to Y
is a cofibration in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
if and only if it is a cofibration in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Reedy}
.

Proof.

We only prove the interesting direction; assume that
X,Y
are cofibrant in
\mathcal{M}_{Loc}^{J}
and that
X\to Y\in\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Reedy}
is a Reedy cofibration. Remains to show that

X_{c}\sqcup_{X_{a}}Y_{a}\to Y_{c}\text{ and }X_{c}\sqcup_{X_{b}}Y_{b}\to Y_{c}

are trivial cofibrations. The fact that the maps are weak equivalences follows by applying the 2-out-of-3 property to the diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{c}\sqcup_{X_{a}}Y_{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{c}\sqcup_{X_{b}}Y_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{c}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

The vertical maps
X_{a}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}X_{c}
,
X_{b}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}X_{c}
,
Y_{a}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Y_{c}
and
Y_{b}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Y_{c}
, are trivial cofibrations since
X
and
Y
are cofibrant in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
. Remains to see that they are cofibrations. From the Reedy condition we have that the map
X_{c}\sqcup_{L_{c}X}L_{c}Y\hookrightarrow Y_{c}
is a cofibration, and observe that the domains of the maps
X_{c}\sqcup_{X_{a}}Y_{a}\to Y_{c}
and
X_{c}\sqcup_{X_{b}}Y_{b}\to Y_{c}
are contained in the colimit
X_{c}\sqcup_{L_{c}X}L_{c}Y
. Therefore, the maps factor as composition of cofibrations

X_{c}\sqcup_{X_{a}}Y_{a}\hookrightarrow X_{c}\sqcup_{L_{c}X}L_{c}Y\hookrightarrow Y_{c}\text{ and }X_{c}\sqcup_{X_{b}}Y_{b}\hookrightarrow X_{c}\sqcup_{L_{c}X}L_{c}Y\hookrightarrow Y_{c},

which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.25
.

Let
X\in\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
cofibrant and
X\to Z\in\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Reedy}
a Reedy trivial cofibration. Then
Z
is cofibrant in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
. Furthermore,
X\to Z
is a trivial cofibration in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
.

Proof.

Since
X\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Z
is a Reedy trivial cofibration, then
X_{a}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Z_{a}
,
X_{b}\sqcup_{X_{a}\sqcup X_{a}}(Z_{a}\sqcup Z_{a})\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Z_{b}
and
X_{c}\sqcup_{(X_{b}\sqcup_{X_{a}}X_{b})}(Z_{b}\sqcup_{Z_{a}}Z_{b})\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Z_{c}
are trivial cofibrations. We then obtain the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{a}\sqcup X_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{a}\sqcup Z_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

This shows that
X_{b}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Z_{b}
is a trivial cofibration. Since
X
is cofibrant then all the maps in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{c}}

are trivial cofibrations. Consider the commutative diagram where the back and front faces are pushouts

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{b}\sqcup_{X_{a}}X_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{b}\sqcup_{Z_{a}}Z_{b},}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

which, by the two-out-of-three, shows that
X_{b}\sqcup_{X_{a}}X_{b}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Z_{b}\sqcup_{Z_{a}}Z_{b}
is a trivial cofibration. Remains to prove that
Z_{b}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Z_{c}
is a trivial cofibration. The pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{b}\sqcup_{X_{a}}X_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{b}\sqcup_{Z_{a}}Z_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

shows that
X_{c}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Z_{c}
is a trivial cofibration. Note that
Z
is Reedy cofibrant, hence
Z_{b}\hookrightarrow Z_{c}
is a cofibration. By the two-out-of-three property, we can conclude that
Z_{b}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Z_{c}
is indeed a trivial cofibration. The above says that
Z
is cofibrant.

The second part is also true, since
X\to Z
is a level-wise weak equivalence.
∎

Corollary 4.26
.

Any map between diagrams
f:X\to Y
, where
X
is a cofibrant diagram
X
and
Y
is a fibrant diagram in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
, can be factored as a trivial cofibration followed by a fibration.

Proof.

We factor
f:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Reedy}
to obtain
X\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Z\twoheadrightarrow Y
.
Z\twoheadrightarrow Y
is also a fibration in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
as is it is level-wise. Finally,
X\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Z\in\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
by the previous
lemma

4.25
.
∎

For the factorization of a diagram map
f:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{M}^{J}
, with
X
cofibrant and
Y
fibrant, into a cofibration followed by a trivial fibration we will need an auxiliary class of diagrams.

Construction 4.27
.

Denote by
K
the category
J
with the opposite Reedy structure given above (the degree function reversed). We endow
\mathcal{M}^{K}
with the Reedy model structure. Then a diagram
Y\in\mathcal{M}^{K}_{Reedy}
is fibrant if
Y_{c}\twoheadrightarrow 1
,
Y_{b}\twoheadrightarrow Y_{c}
and
Y_{a}\twoheadrightarrow Y_{b}\times_{Y_{c}}Y_{b}
are fibrations in
\mathcal{M}
. In this situation
Y_{b}
is also fibrant.

The limit of a diagram
Y\in\mathcal{M}^{K}
is simply the equalizer
Eq(Y_{i},Y_{j})
. Note that the following pullback also computes the limit of
Y
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}\times_{Y_{c}}Y_{b}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

From this we conclude that
\Lim Y
is a fibrant object of
\mathcal{M}
if
Y\in\mathcal{M}^{K}_{Reedy}
is fibrant, and letting
Z
to denote the constant diagram at
\Lim Y
then this comes with a diagram map
Z\to Y
of the following form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lim Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lim Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lim Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{c}}

where all top arrows are identities. Finally, note that
Y
being fibrant in
\mathcal{M}^{K}_{Reedy}
implies that both maps
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}}
are fibrations. This can be deduced from the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}\times_{Y_{c}}Y_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{c}}

Observation 4.28
.

Recall that the fibrations in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
are the level-wise fibrations. Since
Z\in\mathcal{M}^{K}
is point-wise fibrant then it is Reedy fibrant in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
. Similarly,
Y
is Reedy fibrant in
\mathcal{M}^{K}_{Reedy}
, in particular, implies that is object-wise fibrant, so it is fibrant in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
. We will use this diagram
Z
throughout this section.

Lemma 4.29
.

The map
Z\to Y
from above is a trivial fibration in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
.

Proof.

We show that the map has the right lifting property against any cofibration
A\hookrightarrow B\in\mathcal{M}_{Loc}^{J}
. First, assume that
A=\emptyset
, and
B
is a cofibrant object in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
and
Y
a fibrant diagram in
\mathcal{M}^{K}_{Reedy}
. We consider the lifting problem in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}

From the discussion above we obtain the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Thus, we obtain the following lifts:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Yi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{i}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bj}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Yj}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{j}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bk}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Yk}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{k}}

Using this we can construct the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{b}\sqcup_{B_{a}}B_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}\sqcup_{Y_{c}}Y_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

where the middle trivial cofibration and fibration come from
B
being cofibrant in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
and
Y
being fibrant in
\mathcal{M}^{K}_{Reedy}
respectively. Then there exist a map
B_{c}\overset{r}{\to}Y_{a}
that fits in the diagram. Furthermore, we readily see from the diagram that
Y_{j}r=l_{k}=Y_{i}r
. Therefore, there is a unique arrow
B_{c}\overset{t}{\to}Eq(Y_{i},Y_{j})=\Lim Y
making the obvious triangle commutative. By taking the appropriate compositions with the map
t
we can construct a diagram map
B\to Z
such that is a solution to the lifting problem.

For the general case

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}

one can play the same game, the only change is that the diagram is a bit more involved.
∎

The diagram
Z
from
4.27
is not necessarily Reedy cofibrant, but it is almost cofibrant in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
as the maps in it are trivial cofibrations. The only missing part is that
\lim Y
is not cofibrant in
\mathcal{M}
. In order to obtain cofibrant diagram in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
, we include the following result.

Lemma 4.30
.

If
Y\in\mathcal{M}^{K}_{Reedy}
is fibrant then there exists a trivial fibration
W\twoheadrightarrow Y\in\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
with
W\in\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
cofibrant.

Proof.

Since
Y
is fibrant in
\mathcal{M}^{K}_{Reedy}
, then it is fibrant in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
as these are level-wise fibrant. Similarly,
Z
from
4.27
is fibrant in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
, which also comes with a trivial fibration
Z\overset{\sim}{\twoheadrightarrow}Y
by
lemma

4.29
. We can take a Reedy cofibrant replacement
W\overset{\sim}{\twoheadrightarrow}Z
. Since this last map is in particular a level-wise weak equivalence, it implies that the maps in
W
are weak equivalences. By 2-out-of-3 property, the maps in
W
are trivial cofibrations. This makes
W
a cofibrant replacement in
\mathcal{M}^{J}
of
Y
by composing the trivial fibrations
W\overset{\sim}{\twoheadrightarrow}Z\overset{\sim}{\twoheadrightarrow}Y
.
∎

Before giving the factorization, we need a technical result that follows from the next lemma.

Remark 4.31
.

From
[
Hen20
, 2.1.11 Proposition]
, if
A\in\mathcal{M}
is cofibrant then the coslice category
A/\mathcal{M}
inherits a weak model structure from
\mathcal{M}
where a map in
A/\mathcal{M}
is cofibration, fibration and weak equivalences if it is one in
\mathcal{M}
. Dually, one induces a weak model structure on the slice
\mathcal{M}/Y
if
Y
is fibrant.

Construction 4.32
.

Consider a map
f:A\to Y
in
\mathcal{M}
where
A
is cofibrant and
Y
is fibrant. Consider
A/\mathcal{M}
with the weak model structure described in the previous
remark

4.31
.

The map
f:A\to Y
allows us to see
Y
as an object in
A/\mathcal{M}
, which is fibrant as
Y
is fibrant in
\mathcal{M}
. So, we can take the slice
(A/\mathcal{M})/Y
. Objects of
(A/\mathcal{M})/Y
are factorizations of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Let two objects in this category

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

which we refer to as
B
and
X
. A map from
B
to
X
is a diagonal filler of the resulting commutative square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}

A cofibrant object in
(A/\mathcal{M})/Y
is one in which the first map is a cofibration in
\mathcal{M}
, and a fibrant object when the last map is a fibration
i.e.,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

respectively. Also note that the category
(A/\mathcal{M})/Y
coincides with
A/(\mathcal{M}/Y)
, both as categories and as model categories.

Observation 4.33
.

[
Hen20
, 2.4.3 Proposition]
observed that the Quillen adjunction descends to the homotopy categories: If
F:\mathcal{C}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{D}:G
is a Quillen pair, then we obtain a natural isomorphism

\Ho(\mathcal{C}^{\textsc{Bif}})(W,G(Z))\cong\Ho(\mathcal{D}^{\textsc{Bif}})(F(W),Z)

of the homotopy categories.

The category
\Ho(\mathcal{C}^{\textsc{Bif}})
is the localization of the subcategory of bifibrant objects at trivial (co)fibrations. This is the content of
[
Hen20
, 2.2.6 Theorem]
, which also proves that there are equivalences

\Ho(\mathcal{C}^{\textsc{Cof}})\cong\Ho(\mathcal{C}^{\textsc{Bif}})\cong\Ho(\mathcal{C}^{\textsc{Fib}})

where the first category is the localization of
\mathcal{C}^{\textsc{Cof}}
at trivial cofibrations, and the second is the localization of
\mathcal{C}^{\textsc{Fib}}
at trivial fibrations. Therefore, up to these equivalences of categories, we say that
\Ho(F):\Ho(\mathcal{C}^{\textsc{Cof}})\to\Ho(\mathcal{D}^{\textsc{Cof}})
and
\Ho(G):\Ho(\mathcal{D}^{\textsc{Fib}})\to\Ho(\mathcal{C}^{\textsc{Fib}})
are “adjoint”.

Lemma 4.34
.

For all
i:A\hookrightarrow B
and
i^{\prime}:A^{\prime}\hookrightarrow B^{\prime}
cofibrations between cofibrant objects, for all
p:X\twoheadrightarrow Y
fibration between fibrant objects, if there is a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}

then
i\pitchfork p
if and only if
i^{\prime}\pitchfork p
. The dual statement also holds: For all
i:A\hookrightarrow B
core cofibrations, for all
p:X\twoheadrightarrow Y
and
p^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow Y^{\prime}
fibrations between fibrant objects, if there is a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}

then
i\pitchfork p
if and only if
i\pitchfork p^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We prove the first part of the lemma, the second part is dual. We have the following commutative squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}

The proof relies heavily on
4.32
: The middle square above corresponds to a pair of objects
B,X
in a double slice category
A/\mathcal{M}/Y
, and a diagonal filler witnessing that
i\pitchfork p
is a map in this double slice category.

We start with the induced weak model structure on the slice
\mathcal{M}/Y
. Note that from
[
Hen20
, 2.4.2 Example]
the weak equivalence
k:A\to A^{\prime}
induces a weak Quillen equivalence
P_{k}:A/(\mathcal{M}/Y)\leftrightarrows A^{\prime}/(\mathcal{M}/Y):U_{k}
. Observe that
B
,
B^{\prime}
are cofibrant and
Y
is fibrant. In what follows we leave
Y
implicit as we work in the slice
(A/\mathcal{M})/Y
, here we use that
(A/\mathcal{M})/Y=A/(\mathcal{M}/Y)
from
4.32
.

The functor
P_{k}
takes a cofibration
A\hookrightarrow C
along
k:A\to A^{\prime}
, while
U_{k}
precomposes with
k
. Using the following diagram, since
P_{k}B
is cofibrant, by the 2-out-of-3 property

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{k}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

we see that there is a weak equivalence
P_{k}B\overset{\sim}{\to}B^{\prime}
, this implies they are isomorphic in
\Ho(A^{\prime}/(\mathcal{M}/Y))
. We have:

\displaystyle\Hom_{\Ho(A^{\prime}/(\mathcal{M}/Y))}(B^{\prime},X)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\Ho(A^{\prime}/(\mathcal{M}/Y))}(P_{k}(B),X)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\Ho(A/(\mathcal{M}/Y))}(B,U_{k}(X))

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\Ho(A/(\mathcal{M}/Y))}(B,X).

The first isomorphism follows from
B^{\prime}\cong P_{k}(B)
in
\Ho(A^{\prime}/(\mathcal{M}/Y))
, the second is the weak Quillen adjunction
P_{k}\dashv U_{k}
applied to the cofibrant object
B\in(A/\mathcal{M})/Y
and the fibrant object
X\in(A^{\prime}/\mathcal{M})/Y
. We crucially use
4.33
, so the second isomorphism is really up to some equivalence of categories.

Now we use
\Hom_{\Ho(A^{\prime}/(\mathcal{M}/Y))}(B^{\prime},X)\cong\Hom_{\Ho(A/(\mathcal{M}/Y))}(B,X)
to conclude. First, recall that a diagonal filler of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}

is the same as a map
B\to X
in
A/\mathcal{M}/Y
, and similarly for
B^{\prime}
and
X
. Assume that
i\pitchfork p
, this give us a map
B\to X
in
\Ho(A/\mathcal{M}/Y)
. Using the isomorphism, we have a map
B^{\prime}\to X
in
\Ho(A^{\prime}/\mathcal{M}/Y)
, from which we can select a representative of the homotopy class, which implies that
i^{\prime}\pitchfork p
. Similarly, we get that
i^{\prime}\pitchfork p
implies
i\pitchfork p
.
∎

Lemma 4.35
.

Let
X\to Y
be a map in
\mathcal{M}^{J}
with
X
cofibrant and
Y
fibrant. Then such a map can be factored as a cofibration followed by a trivial fibration.

Proof.

Observe first that
Y
can be assumed to be Reedy cofibrant in
\mathcal{M}^{J}
. Indeed, we can simply take a Reedy cofibrant replacement
Y^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\twoheadrightarrow}Y
, and instead use the dashed arrow

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Under this assumption,
Y
is point-wise cofibrant, whence Reedy cofibrant in
\mathcal{M}^{K}
. Therefore, we can take a fibrant replacement in
\mathcal{M}^{K}
,
Y\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Y^{\prime}
. Using
[
Hen20
, Corollary 2.4.4]
equivalences are preserved under pullbacks along fibrations, so we get the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\prime}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Furthermore, we know from
lemma

4.30
that
W\twoheadrightarrow Y^{\prime}
is a trivial fibration in
\mathcal{M}^{J}
. Therefore, it has the right lifting property against any cofibration between cofibrant objects in
\mathcal{M}^{J}
. We can use
lemma

4.34
to conclude that
LY\twoheadrightarrow Y
satisfies the same property,
i.e.,
it is a trivial fibration in
\mathcal{M}^{J}
. Since
X
is cofibrant, we obtain a lift

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

The map
X\to LY
can be factored in the Reedy model structure
\mathcal{M}^{J}
as
X\hookrightarrow X^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\twoheadrightarrow}LY
. The diagram
X^{\prime}
is cofibrant in
\mathcal{M}^{J}
since it is equivalent to the cofibrant diagram
LY
, and
X
is cofibrant by assumption. Therefore, it follows from
lemma

4.25
that the Reedy cofibration
X\hookrightarrow X^{\prime}
is a cofibration in the model
\mathcal{M}^{J}
. This gives us the desired factorization in
\mathcal{M}^{J}
,
X\hookrightarrow X^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\twoheadrightarrow}Y
.
∎

All the previous work can be summarized in the following proof of
theorem

4.19
. This proves that the category of diagrams
\mathcal{M}^{J}
has a weak model structure with the specified cofibrations and fibrations, which, as explained above, encodes objects with a weak cylinder object. We remark that our proof will show that the conditions of
[
Hen20
, 2.1.10 Definition]
are satisfied instead of
definition

C.1
. The reason is for this is that in
theorem

4.19
we do not have an explicit class of weak equivalences. More precisely, we will use
[
Hen20
, 2.3.3 Proposition]
which gives some alternative criteria to obtain a weak model structure in this sense.

Proof.

(
theorem

4.19
)
Note first that we have the Reedy weak model structure on
\mathcal{M}^{J}
by virtue of
theorem

C.11
. Also, the existence of initial and terminal diagrams is clear. We must justify that the class of (co)fibrations form a class of (co)fibrations in
\mathcal{M}^{J}
. For fibrations, since these are level-wise, it is immediate that: the terminal diagram is fibrant, any isomorphism with fibrant codomain is a fibration, the class is closed under compositions, and stable under pullbacks along maps between fibrant objects.

The dual conditions must be verified for the class of cofibrations. That the initial diagram is cofibrant it is immediate to verify. To see other stability conditions, we observe these are true for
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Reedy}
. In addition, for stability under isomorphisms we use repeatedly that maps in
\mathcal{M}
isomorphic to trivial cofibration are also trivial cofibrations. This simply because the new condition we added involves the requirement that certain maps trivial cofibrations. Stability under pushouts follows from the stability in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Reedy}
and the fact that trivial cofibrations in the weak model
\mathcal{M}
are pushout stable.

The factorization of a map
f:X\to Y
, where
X
is cofibrant and
Y
is fibrant, into a cofibration followed by a trivial fibration is the content of
lemma

4.35
.

The factorization of a map
f:X\to Y
, where
X
is cofibrant and
Y
is fibrant, into a trivial cofibration followed by a fibration is guaranteed by
corollary

4.26
.

In order to conclude, we use
[
Hen20
, 2.3.3 Proposition]
. For which we need to verify that a cofibration
X\to Y\in\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
with
X
cofibrant and
Y
fibrant admit a relative strong cylinder object. Firstly, we know that the map admits a relative cylinder object in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Reedy}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\coprod_{X}Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I_{X}Y}}

with
Y\hookrightarrow Y\coprod_{X}Y\hookrightarrow I_{X}Y
a Reedy trivial cofibration. Since
Y
is cofibrant in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
we can use
lemma

4.25
to conclude that
I_{X}Y
is also cofibrant in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
, and that the map
Y\to I_{X}Y
is a trivial cofibration in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
. Now we have cofibrant objects
Y\coprod_{X}Y
,
I_{X}Y
in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
and a Reedy cofibration between them, so we use
lemma

4.24
to conclude it is actually a cofibration in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
. This gives us the relative cylinder objects.

Finally, the 2-out-of-3 property for trivial cofibrations between bifibrant objects follows using that
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Reedy}
is a weak model category, so the property is true in this Reedy weak model structure. By which we mean that the property is true for the underlying Reedy trivial cofibrations between bifibrant objects of
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
.
Lemma

4.25
allows us to conclude that such Reedy trivial cofibrations are indeed trivial cofibrations in
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
. Now
[
Hen20
, 2.3.3 Proposition]
allows us to conclude that
\mathcal{M}^{J}_{Loc}
, with the specified classes of maps, is a weak model category.
∎

4.3.2
Weak model on correspondences

Next, we consider another diagram category
I
:

0\to 2\leftarrow 1

Where
\deg(0)=\deg(1)=0
and
\deg(2)=1
. Similarly to the previous section, we construct a “right Bousfield localization” of the Reedy weak model structure on
\mathcal{N}^{I}
.

Theorem 4.36
.

There is a weak model structure
\mathcal{N}^{I}_{Loc}
on the category of diagrams
\mathcal{N}^{I}
obtained from the Reedy weak model structure
\mathcal{N}^{I}_{Reedy}
, where:

1.

A map between diagrams
X\to Y
is a cofibration if

(a)

It is a Reedy cofibration,

(b)

X_{2}\sqcup_{X_{1}}Y_{1}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Y_{2}
and
X_{2}\sqcup_{X_{0}}Y_{0}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}Y_{2}
are trivial cofibrations in
\mathcal{N}
.

2.

Fibrations are level-wise fibrations.

It will be useful to have in mind that for an object
X\in\mathcal{N}^{I}
we have
L_{0}X=0
and
L_{1}X=X_{0}\sqcup X_{1}
. So a map
X\to Y
is a Reedy cofibration if the maps
X_{0}\hookrightarrow Y_{0}
,
X_{1}\hookrightarrow Y_{1}
and
(Y_{0}\sqcup Y_{1})\sqcup_{(X_{0}\sqcup X_{1})}X_{2}\hookrightarrow Y_{2}
are cofibrations.

Observation 4.37
.

Unwinding the definitions, a diagram
X\in\mathcal{N}^{I}_{Loc}
is cofibrant if both maps
X_{0}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}X_{2}
and
X_{1}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}X_{2}
are trivial cofibrations.

The proof of the theorem is completely analogous to
theorem

4.19
. We state the lemmas necessary for this and only comment on the proofs when adequate.

Lemma 4.38
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathcal{N}^{I}_{Loc}
cofibrant. Then, a map
X\to Y
is a cofibration in
\mathcal{N}^{I}_{Loc}
if and only if it is a cofibration in
\mathcal{N}^{I}_{Reedy}
.

Proof.

Just as in
lemma

4.24
we only prove the interesting direction; assume that
X,Y
are cofibrant in
\mathcal{N}_{Loc}^{I}
and that
X\to Y\in\mathcal{N}^{I}_{Reedy}
is a Reedy cofibration. Remains to show that

X_{2}\sqcup_{X_{0}}Y_{0}\to Y_{2}\text{ and }X_{2}\sqcup_{X_{1}}Y_{1}\to Y_{2}

are trivial cofibrations. Again, the fact that the maps are weak equivalences follow from
X,Y
being cofibrant and the 2-out-of-3 property. To see that they are cofibrations we can use the Reedy condition just as in
lemma

4.24
.
∎

Lemma 4.39
.

Let
X\in\mathcal{N}^{I}_{Loc}
cofibrant and
X\to Z\in\mathcal{N}^{I}_{Reedy}
a Reedy trivial cofibration. Then
Z
is cofibrant in
\mathcal{N}^{I}_{Loc}
. Furthermore,
X\to Z
is a trivial cofibration in
\mathcal{N}^{I}_{Loc}
.

Proof.

The difficult part is to show that
Z
is cofibrant. Since
X\to Z
is a Reedy trivial cofibration, then by
corollary

C.16
we have it is a levelwise trivial cofibration. Then
Z
is cofibrant by the 2-out-of-3 property.
∎

Corollary 4.40
.

Any map between diagrams
f:X\to Y
, where
X
is a cofibrant diagram and
Y
is a fibrant diagram in
\mathcal{N}^{I}_{Loc}
, can be factored as a trivial cofibration followed by a fibration.

Proof.

Now that we have
lemma

4.39
, we can proceed as in
corollary

4.26
by first taking the factorization in
\mathcal{N}^{I}_{Reedy}
.
∎

Construction 4.41
.

Denote by
K^{\prime}
the category
I
with the opposite Reedy structure given above (the degree function reversed). We endow
\mathcal{N}^{K^{\prime}}
with the Reedy model structure. Then a diagram
Y\in\mathcal{N}^{K^{\prime}}_{Reedy}
is fibrant if
Y_{2}\twoheadrightarrow 1
,
Y_{0}\twoheadrightarrow Y_{2}
and
Y_{1}\twoheadrightarrow Y_{2}
are fibrations in
\mathcal{N}
.

In this situation we can see that
\lim Y=Y_{0}\times_{Y_{2}}Y_{1}
and is fibrant in
\mathcal{N}
. We can again take a
Z\in\mathcal{N}^{I}
to be the correspondence with constant value
\lim Y
. So it comes with a map
Z\to Y
.

Lemma 4.42
.

The map
Z\to Y
from above is a trivial fibration in
\mathcal{N}^{I}_{Loc}
.

Proof.

The same idea as in
lemma

4.29
carries over here. The diagrams are even simpler.
∎

Lemma 4.43
.

If
Y\in\mathcal{N}^{K^{\prime}}_{Reedy}
is fibrant then there exists a trivial fibration
W\twoheadrightarrow Y\in\mathcal{N}^{I}_{Loc}
with
W\in\mathcal{N}^{I}_{Loc}
cofibrant.

Proof.

The argument of
lemma

4.30
applies here too.
∎

Lemma 4.44
.

Let
X\to Y
be a map in
\mathcal{N}^{I}
with
X
cofibrant and
Y
fibrant. Then such a map can be factored as a cofibration followed by a trivial fibration.

Proof.

We have all ingredients to proceed as in
lemma

4.35
. Firstly, we can assume that
Y
is Reedy cofibrant in
\mathcal{N}^{I}
and we can take a fibrant replacement in
\mathcal{N}^{K}
. So we can construct the following pullback square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\prime}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Then we can obtain a map
X\to LY
. Factoring this map as
X\hookrightarrow X^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\twoheadrightarrow}LY
, the first map is moreover a cofibration in
\mathcal{N}^{I}_{Loc}
in view of
lemma

4.39
. This produces the factorization
X\hookrightarrow X^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\twoheadrightarrow}Y
.
∎

The proof of
theorem

4.36
is a carbon copy from the one of
theorem

4.19
, the lemmas of this section provide us with all the required steps.

4.3.3
Projections are Barton trivial fibrations

Lemma 4.45
.

The functor
\mathcal{N}^{I}\to\mathcal{N}
such that
A\to B\leftarrow C\in\mathcal{N}^{I}\mapsto A\in\mathcal{N}
, is extensible. Also, the functor
\mathcal{N}^{I}\to\mathcal{N}
such that
A\to B\leftarrow C\in\mathcal{N}^{I}\mapsto C\in\mathcal{N}
is extensible.

Proof.

Let
A\coloneqq a\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}b\overset{\sim}{\hookleftarrow}c\in\mathcal{N}^{I}_{Loc}
be a cofibrant diagram and
x\in\mathcal{N}^{\textsc{Cof}}
a cofibrant object and a cofibration
a\hookrightarrow x
. We take the fibrant replacement of
x
and consider the pushout as indicated below, and we obtain a solution to the lifting problem on the right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{fib}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b\sqcup_{a}x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

The resulting map
c\to x^{fib}
can be factored as
c\hookrightarrow z\overset{\sim}{\twoheadrightarrow}x^{fib}
. We can take further pushouts

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{fib}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b\sqcup_{a}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z\sqcup_{c}b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

There is a map
P\to x^{fib}
which we can factor as
P\hookrightarrow y\overset{\sim}{\twoheadrightarrow}x^{fib}
, and the resulting diagram we get

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{fib}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b\sqcup_{a}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z\sqcup_{c}b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Furthermore, there is a map
b\sqcup_{a}x\to y
which is a cofibration as it is the composite of the two cofibrations. Using the 2-out-of-3 property repeatedly, one concludes that the map
z\sqcup_{c}b\to y
is a trivial cofibration. Thus, we have constructed the cofibrant object
X\coloneqq z\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}y\overset{\sim}{\hookleftarrow}x\in\mathcal{N}^{I}_{Loc}
. The induced map
A\to X
is a level-wise cofibration. The maps
b\sqcup_{a}x\to y
and
b\sqcup_{a}z\to y
are trivial cofibrations.

Remains to show that
A\to X
is a Reedy cofibration. We already have that
a\to x
and
c\to z
are cofibrations. We now need to show that the induced map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a\sqcup c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x\sqcup z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(x\sqcup z)\sqcup_{a\sqcup c}b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}

is a cofibration. By diagram chasing, one can show that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a\sqcup c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x\sqcup z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(z\sqcup_{c}b)\sqcup_{b}(b\sqcup_{a}x)}}

commutes. One shows that the bottom right corner computes the pushout of the span. Using that the map
P\hookrightarrow y
is a cofibration one concludes that
(x\sqcup)\sqcup_{a\sqcup c}b\to y
is also a cofibration. This concludes the proof that
A\to X
is a Reedy core cofibration in
\mathcal{N}^{I}
. Therefore, it must a cofibration. We summarize our construction with the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

This cofibration is a (strict) lift of
a\hookrightarrow x
, showing that the functor
\mathcal{N}^{I}\to N
is an extensible functor. The second part of the lemma is analogous.
∎

Observation 4.46
.

Note that in the previous
lemma

4.45
, using 2-out-of-3 property, if we start with a trivial cofibration
a\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}x
then we obtain a level-wise equivalence between cofibrant objects in
\mathcal{N}^{I}_{Loc}
. We conclude that the projections are weakly conservative.

Corollary 4.47
.

The functor
\mathcal{N}^{I}\to\mathcal{N}
such that
A\to B\leftarrow C\in\mathcal{N}^{I}\mapsto A\in\mathcal{N}
, is a Barton trivial fibration. Also, the functor
\mathcal{N}^{I}\to\mathcal{N}
such that
A\to B\leftarrow C\in\mathcal{N}^{I}\mapsto C\in\mathcal{N}
, is a Barton trivial fibration.

Proof.

We saw in
lemma

4.45
that the projections are extensible and from
4.46
that is weakly conservative. It is also straightforward to see that it preserve cofibrations and trivial cofibrations.
∎

We now want to see that any left Quillen functor
F:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
part of a Quillen equivalence between weak model categories admits a Brown-like factorization. To this end, consider the following:

Construction 4.48
.

We define the category of diagrams

\mathcal{N}_{F}^{I}\coloneqq\{Fa\to b\leftarrow c|a\in\mathcal{M}^{\textsc{Cof}},\,b,c\in\mathcal{N}\}.

The weak model structure on this category is similar to that of
\mathcal{N}^{I}
, the only difference is that
X\to Y
is a cofibration if
X_{b}\sqcup_{FX_{a}}FY_{a}\to Y_{b}
is a trivial cofibration.

When
F
is the identity functor we recover
\mathcal{N}^{I}
from
theorem

4.36
. A cofibrant object in
\mathcal{N}_{F}^{I}
is a diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fa}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Observation 4.49
.

With the set up above, it follows from
corollary

4.47
that the projection
\pi_{1}:\mathcal{N}_{F}^{I}\to\mathcal{M}
, sending each diagram
Fa\to b\leftarrow c
to
a
, is a Barton trivial fibration.

To show that the projection from
\pi_{2}:\mathcal{N}_{F}^{I}\to\mathcal{N}
sending each diagram
Fa\to b\leftarrow c
to
c\in\mathcal{N}
is a trivial fibration we make use of the following:

Lemma 4.50
.

Let
F:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
be a left Quillen equivalence between weak model categories. For any objects
x\in\mathcal{M}^{\textsc{Cof}}
,
y\in\mathcal{N}^{\textsc{Fib}}
and a map
f:Fx\to y
there exists an object
z\in\mathcal{M}^{\textsc{Cof}}
such that
f
factors as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fz.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Proof.

We know that there is an isomorphism

\varphi:\Hom_{\mathcal{N}}(Fx,y)\simeq\Hom_{\mathcal{M}}(x,Gy):\varphi^{-1}

given by the Quillen adjunction, natural in
x\in\mathcal{M}^{\textsc{Cof}}
and
y\in\mathcal{N}^{\textsc{Fib}}
. Recall from
[
Hen20
, 2.4.3 Proposition]
that
F:\mathcal{M}^{\textsc{Cof}}\to\mathcal{N}^{\textsc{Cof}}
and
G:\mathcal{N}^{\textsc{Fib}}\to\mathcal{M}^{\textsc{Fib}}
preserve equivalences. Take
\varphi f
the adjoint transpose of
f
. We can take a factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gy}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi f}

By naturality, one checks that
f=\varphi^{-1}sFr
where
Fr
is a cofibration. Since the Quillen pair is an equivalence, we deduce from
[
Hen20
, 2.4.5 Proposition (i)]
that
\varphi^{-1}s
is an equivalence.
∎

Corollary 4.51
.

Let
F:\mathcal{M}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{N}:G
be a Quillen equivalence. Then the projection
\pi_{2}:\mathcal{N}_{F}^{I}\to\mathcal{N}
sending each diagram
Fa\to b\leftarrow c
to
c\in\mathcal{N}
is a Barton trivial fibration.

Proof.

We show that in a situation as in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fa}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

there is a cofibrant object over
z
that projects onto
c\hookrightarrow z
. By taking a fibrant replacement, we can assume that the diagram is point-wise fibrant. From
[
Hen20
, 2.2.3 Proposition]
there exists a homotopy inverse of
c\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}b
, this give us a map
Fa\to c
. Using
lemma

4.50
this last map can be factored as
Fa\hookrightarrow Fx\overset{\sim}{\to}c
. The rest of the proof continues as in
corollary

4.47
.
∎

Theorem 4.52
.

Given
F:\mathcal{M}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{N}
be a left Quillen equivalence between weak model categories. Then, we have a diagram of week model categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{N}^{I}_{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{N},}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Id_{\mathcal{M}},F)}

where
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are Barton trivial fibrations.

Proof.

The work we have done produces a diagram as on the left below, and the action of the functors on objects is spelled out on the right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{N}^{I}_{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{a}\rightrightarrows X_{b}\to X_{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{FX_{a}\rightrightarrows FX_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(X_{a},FX_{a})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Id_{\mathcal{M}},F)}

We have shown in
corollary

4.47
and
corollary

4.51
that both projections are Barton trivial fibrations.
∎

It will be essential to highlight that there is a diagonal functor which is a Barton trivial fibration, making the lower triangle commutative.

Corollary 4.53
.

Let
F:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
be a left Quillen equivalence. There exists a Barton trivial fibration
P:\mathcal{N}_{F}^{I}\to\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.

Theorem

4.52
can be further specialized to a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{N}^{I}_{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Id_{\mathcal{M}}}

from which we see that there is a functor
P:\mathcal{N}^{I}_{F}\to\mathcal{M}
. This is an immediate consequence of
theorem

4.52
.
∎

4.4
Proof of main theorem

Theorem 4.54
.

Let
F:\mathcal{M}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{N}:G
a Quillen equivalence. Then, for any cofibrant object
A\in\mathcal{M}
. The induced map
h\mathbb{L}F_{A}:h\mathbb{L}_{\lambda}^{\mathcal{M}}(A)\to h\mathbb{L}_{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}}(FA)
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Recall from
lemma

4.8
that for any cofibrant object
A
the induced map
h\mathbb{L}F_{A}
is injective. Remains to show that it is surjective.
Using
corollary

4.53
, we obtain a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{N}^{I}_{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

where
P
is a Barton trivial fibration.
P:\mathcal{N}_{F}^{I}\to\mathcal{M}
induces, for any cofibrant object
X\in\mathcal{N}_{F}^{I}
, an isomorphism
(h\mathbb{L}\pi_{1})_{X}:h\mathbb{L}_{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}_{F}^{I}}(X)\to h\mathbb{L}_{\lambda}^{\mathcal{M}}(\pi_{1}X)
. Indeed, this follows from
theorem

4.16
. Similarly, the map
(h\mathbb{L}\pi_{2})_{X}:h\mathbb{L}_{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}_{F}^{I}}(X)\to h\mathbb{L}_{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}}(\pi_{2}X)
is an isomorphism of
\lambda
-boolean algebras. For
A\in\mathcal{M}^{\textsc{Cof}}
cofibrant we can get a correspondence in
C_{FA}\in\mathcal{N}^{I}_{F}
with all objects
FA
and maps the identities. We can conclude that
h\mathbb{L}F_{A}
is surjective by chasing through the maps
(h\mathbb{L}\pi_{2})_{C_{A}}
and
(h\mathbb{L}P)_{C_{A}}
which we already know are isomorphisms.
∎

It is an immediate that:

Corollary 4.55
.

For any Quillen equivalence
F:\mathcal{M}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{N}:G
. The functors
Ho(F)\circ h\mathbb{L}_{\lambda}^{\mathcal{M}}
and
h\mathbb{L}_{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}}:Ho(\mathcal{N})\to\mathbf{Bool}_{\lambda}
are naturally isomorphic via
h\mathbb{L}F
.

Appendix A
Infinitary Cartmell theories

We introduce a generalization of
Cartmell theories
, also known as
generalized algebraic theories
, Cartmell
[
Car78
]
. This is straightforward and most of the proofs will be omitted since they are similar to those in
[
Car78
]
. In very few cases we will need to provide new proofs. We claim no originality other than the generalization itself.
We begin by recalling some definitions given in
Ibid
. We assume to have a set of variables
V
whose size is
\aleph_{0}
and an alphabet
A
. Informally, a
Cartmell generalized algebraic theory
consists of:

i)

A set
S
, called the set of
sort symbols
,

ii)

A set
O
, called the set of
operation symbols
,

iii)

An introductory rule for each sort symbol,

iv)

An introductory rule for each operation symbol,

v)

A set of axioms.

To understand our generalization let us examine the previous definition in more detail, for this we need some preliminary notions. An
expression
is a finite sequence of
A\cup V\cup\{(\}\cup\{)\}\cup\{,\}
. Inductively:

i)

Elements of
V
and
A
are expressions,

ii)

If
f\in A
and
e_{1},\,e_{2},...,e_{n}
are expressions, then
f(e_{1},\,e_{2},...,\,e_{n})
is an expression.

The set of expressions is denoted by
E
. This is simply to say that an expression is a finite string taken from the set
A\cup V\cup\{(\}\cup\{)\}\cup\{,\}
. A
premise
is a finite (possibly empty) sequence of
V\times E
. A
conclusion
is an n-tuple of expressions, i.e. any element of
E^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Finally, a
rule
is given by a premise
P
and a conclusion
C
. Rules are written as:
P\vdash C
. This intends to convey the idea that under the premise
P
, the conclusion
C
is a valid expression.
Whenever
P
is a premise we will write
x_{1}:\Delta_{1},\,x_{2}:\Delta_{2},...,x_{n}:\Delta_{n}
. For a conclusion, this is slightly more involved since we differentiate depending on the size of the tuple. For example, if we have a 1-tuple
\Delta
, then we write
\Delta\textup{{Type}}
. We favour the notation “:” from type theory instead of the set theoretic one “
\epsilon
” used by Cartmell. Furthermore, we will take advantage of conventions and notation from type theory.

The most important definition we will need to change is that of a
context
. In a Cartmell theory, a
context
is the premise such that a rule

x_{1}:\Delta_{1},\,x_{2}:\Delta_{2}(x_{1}),...,x_{n}:\Delta_{n}(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n-1})\vdash\Delta(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n})\,\textup{{Type}}

is a
derived rule
.

The only difference between Cartmell theories and infinitary Cartmell theories is that in we allow infinitely many variables in the contexts. Just as any Cartmell theory gives rise to a contextual category, the same is true for the infinitary case with the appropriate generalized version of a contextual category.

A.1
Generalized algebraic theories

In this section, we give the formal definition of an infinitary Cartmell theory. We follow Cartmell
[
Car78
]
to develop the theory; however, there will be some instances where a change has to be made. We could say that by changing in the definition every instance of “finite” by “size strictly less than
\kappa
” we get the correct notion, this is indeed the case. We carve out the definition with a fair amount of detail, since the applications we have in mind benefit from having an explicit syntax. The technicalities and motivations for introducing a generalized algebraic in the following way are presented in Cartmell
[
Car78
]
.

From now on, we fix a regular cardinal
\kappa
, unless otherwise stated, all other ordinals mentioned will be strictly smaller than
\kappa
.

Let
V
be a set such that
|V|=\kappa
, this set will be called the set of
variables
. We make an additional assumption on this set: Its elements have
canonical names
, this is
V=\{x_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\kappa}
. This is also known as an
enumeration
. This is a minor assumption that allows to change variables. Otherwise, we would need to prove a result similar to
[
Car78
, Corollary, pp 1.32]
5
5

5

This result states that under the substitution property the derived rules are stable under substitution of variables by another variables
.
Let
A
be any set, which as before is called
alphabet
. Following
[
Car78
]
we define inductively the collection of
expressions

A^{*}
over the alphabet
A
. An expression is any
\lambda
-sequence of
A\cup V\cup\{(\}\cup\{)\}\cup\{,\}
subject to:

i)

If
x_{\alpha}\in V
then
x_{\alpha}\in A^{*}
,

ii)

If
F\in A
then
F\in A^{*}
,

iii)

If
F\in A
and
\{e_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\subseteq A^{*}
then
F(e_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}\in A^{*}
.

A
premise
is any
\lambda
-sequence of
V\times A^{*}
. We will usually write premises as
\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}
where
x_{\alpha}
are variables and
\Delta_{\alpha}
are expressions for
\alpha<\lambda
. Suppose we have a premise
\Gamma
, or later a
context
, and we need an extra premise (or
context
), according to our variable numbering, formally, we must write
\Gamma,\,\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\lambda\leq\alpha<\mu}
, where
\lambda
represents the number of variables in
\Gamma
. This is clearly a problem when the expression complexity increases. In order to avoid overloading the notation, we choose to reset the variable counting to only the essential variables in use. Under this convention, we will write
\Gamma,\,\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}
instead. We will freely assume that
\Gamma
is a premise unless otherwise specified.

Definition A.1
.

A
judgment
is an expression over the alphabet
A
that has one of the following forms:

1.

Type judgment:
\Gamma\vdash\Delta\,\textup{{Type}}.

2.

Element judgment:
\Gamma\vdash t:\Delta.

3.

Type equality judgment:
\Gamma\vdash\Delta\equiv\Delta^{\prime}.

4.

Term equality judgment:
\Gamma\vdash t\equiv_{\Delta}t^{\prime}.

where
\Gamma
is a premise.

Given a premise
\Gamma
,
\{e_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}
expression and
\{x_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}
variables then the new expression

\Gamma[e_{\alpha}|x_{\alpha}]_{\alpha<\lambda}

it is obtained by simultaneously changing the variables in
\Gamma
by the expressions. This process, unsurprisingly, is called
substitution
of variables. Along with the infinitary substitutions, we will also allow operations to have possibly infinite arity. This is made explicit:

Definition A.2
.

A
\kappa
-
pretheory

T
consists of the following data:

i)

A set
S
, called the set of
sort symbols
,

ii)

A set
O
, called the set of
operation symbols
,

iii)

For each sort symbol
B
, a judgment of the form:

\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash B(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}\,\textup{{Type}}

where
\lambda
is some ordinal strictly smaller than
\kappa
,

iv)

For each operator symbol
F
, a judgment:

\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash F(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}:\Delta

where
\lambda
is an ordinal strictly smaller than
\kappa
,

v)

A set of judgments, each of which is either a type equality judgment or a term equality judgment, listed in
definition

A.1
. This is the set of
axioms
of the
\kappa
-pretheory.

The following definitions are of inductive nature:

Definition A.3
.

1.

A premise
\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}
is a
context
if the judgment

\{x_{\beta}:\Delta_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\alpha}\vdash\Delta_{\alpha}\,\textup{{Type}}

is a
derived judgment
of
T
for every
\alpha<\lambda
. Whenever we want to specify that a premise
\Gamma
is a context we will write
\vdash\Gamma\,\textup{{Ctxt}}
.

2.

The judgment

\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash\Delta\,\textup{{Type}}

is a
well-formed judgment
of
T
if and only if
\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}
is a context.

3.

The judgment

\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash t:\Delta

is
well-formed
if and only if

\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash\Delta\,\textup{{Type}}

is a
derived judgment
of
T
.

Definition A.4
.

Let
T
be a
\kappa
-pretheory. The set of
derived judgments
of
T
are the ones that can be derived from the following list of rules:

1.

\frac{\Gamma\vdash A\,\textup{{Type}}}{\Gamma\vdash A\equiv A}

2.

\frac{\Gamma\vdash t:A}{\Gamma\vdash t\equiv_{A}t}

3.

\frac{\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\equiv A_{2}}{\Gamma\vdash A_{2}\equiv A_{1}}

4.

\frac{\Gamma\vdash t_{1}\equiv_{A}t_{2}}{\Gamma\vdash t_{2}\equiv_{A}t_{1}}

5.

\frac{\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\equiv A_{2}\quad\Gamma\vdash A_{2}\equiv A_{3}}{\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\equiv A_{3}}

6.

\frac{\Gamma\vdash t_{1}\equiv_{A}t_{2}\quad\Gamma\vdash t_{2}\equiv_{A}t_{3}}{\Gamma\vdash t_{1}\equiv_{A}t_{3}}

7.

\frac{\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\equiv A_{2}\quad\Gamma\vdash t_{1}\equiv_{A_{1}}t_{2}}{\Gamma\vdash t_{2}\equiv_{A_{2}}t_{1}}

8.

\frac{\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\equiv A_{2}\quad\Gamma\vdash t:A_{1}}{\Gamma\vdash t:A_{2}}

9.

\frac{\Gamma,\,\{x_{\delta}:A_{\delta}\}_{\delta<\beta<\lambda}\vdash A_{\beta}\,\textup{{Type}}}{\Gamma,\,\{x_{\alpha}:A_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash x_{\alpha}:A_{\alpha}}

10.

For any
B
sort symbol with a well-formed introduction type judgment:

\frac{\{x_{\alpha}:A_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash B(x_{\lambda})\,\textup{{Type}},\quad\vdash\Gamma\,\textup{{Ctxt}},\quad\Gamma\vdash t_{\alpha}:B[t_{\alpha}|x_{\alpha}]}{\Gamma\vdash B(t_{\lambda})\,\textup{{Type}}}

11.

For any
F
operator symbol with a well-formed introduction type element judgment:

\frac{\Gamma,\,\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash F(x_{\lambda}):\Delta,\quad\Gamma\vdash t_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}[t_{\alpha}|x_{\alpha}]}{\Gamma,\,\{t_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}[t_{\alpha}|x_{\alpha}]\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash F(t_{\lambda}):\Delta[t_{\lambda}\mid x_{\lambda}]}

12.

\frac{\vdash\Gamma\,\textup{{Ctxt}}\quad\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash\Delta\equiv\Delta^{\prime}\quad\quad\quad\Gamma,\,t_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}[t_{\beta}\mid x_{\beta}]_{\beta<\alpha},\,t_{\alpha}^{\prime}:\Delta_{\alpha}^{\prime}[t_{\beta}^{\prime}\mid x_{\beta}]_{\beta<\alpha}\vdash t_{\alpha}\equiv_{\Delta_{\alpha}[t_{\beta}|x_{\beta}]_{\beta<\alpha}}t_{\alpha}^{\prime}}{\Gamma,\,\{t_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}[t_{\beta}\mid x_{\beta}]_{\beta<\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda},\,\{t_{\alpha}^{\prime}:\Delta_{\alpha}^{\prime}[t_{\beta}^{\prime}\mid x_{\beta}]_{\beta<\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\\
\\
\vdash\Delta[t_{\alpha}|x_{\alpha}]_{\alpha<\lambda}\equiv\Delta^{\prime}[t_{\alpha}^{\prime}|x_{\alpha}]_{\alpha<\lambda}}

13.

\frac{\vdash\Gamma\,\textup{{Ctxt}}\quad\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash t\equiv_{\Delta}t^{\prime}\quad\quad\quad\Gamma,\,s_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}[s_{\beta}\mid x_{\beta}]_{\beta<\alpha},\,s_{\alpha}^{\prime}:\Delta_{\alpha}[s_{\beta}^{\prime}\mid x_{\beta}]_{\beta<\alpha}\vdash s_{\alpha}\equiv_{\Delta_{\alpha}[s_{\beta}^{\prime}\mid x_{\beta}]_{\beta<\alpha}}s_{\alpha}^{\prime}}{\Gamma,\,\{s_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}[s_{\beta}\mid x_{\beta}]_{\beta<\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda},\,\{s_{\alpha}^{\prime}:\Delta_{\alpha}[s_{\beta}^{\prime}\mid x_{\beta}]_{\beta<\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\\
\\
\vdash t[s_{\alpha}\mid x_{\alpha}]_{\alpha<\lambda}\equiv_{\Delta[s_{\alpha}\mid x_{\alpha}]_{\alpha<\lambda}}t^{\prime}[s_{\alpha}^{\prime}\mid x_{\alpha}]_{\alpha<\lambda}}

14.

If
\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash\Delta\equiv\Delta^{\prime}
is an axiom then

\frac{\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash\Delta\,\textup{{Type}}\quad\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash\Delta^{\prime}\,\textup{{Type}},}{\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash\Delta\equiv\Delta^{\prime}}

15.

If
\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash t\equiv_{\Delta}t^{\prime}
is an axiom then

\frac{\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash t:\Delta\quad\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash t^{\prime}:\Delta}{\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash t\equiv_{\Delta}t^{\prime}}

We are now ready for the following:

Definition A.5
.

A
\kappa
-pretheory
T
is
well-formed
if all its rules are well-formed. A
generalized
\kappa
-algebraic theory
is a well-formed
\kappa
-pretheory.

Remark A.6
.

Observe that a generalized algebraic theory as defined by Cartmell
[
Car78
]
is the same as an
\omega
-generalized algebraic theory in our sense.

We introduce an important example of
\kappa
-algebraic theories.

Example A.7
.

Let
Cat
denote the
\omega
-algebraic theory defined in the following way:

1.

Type of objects:
\vdash\,\textup{{Ob}}\,\textup{{Type}}
.

2.

Type of morphisms:
x:\textup{{Ob}},\,y:\textup{{Ob}}\vdash\,\textup{{Hom}}(x,y)\,\textup{{Type}}
.

3.

Composition operation:
x:\textup{{Ob}},\,y:\textup{{Ob}},\,z:\textup{{Ob}},\,f:\textup{{Hom}}(x,y),\,g:\textup{{Hom}}(y,z)\vdash g\circ f:\textup{{Hom}}(x,z)
.

4.

Identity operator:
x:\textup{{Ob}}\vdash\,\textup{{id}}_{x}:\textup{{Hom}}(x,x)
.

Subject to the following axioms:

\frac{x:\textup{{Ob}},\,y:\textup{{Ob}},\,f:\textup{{Hom}}(x,y)}{\textup{{id}}_{y}\circ f\equiv f}\quad\quad\frac{x:\textup{{Ob}},\,y:\textup{{Ob}},f:\textup{{Hom}}(x,y)}{f\circ\textup{{id}}_{x}\equiv f}

\frac{x:\textup{{Ob}},\,y::\textup{{Ob}},\,z:\textup{{Ob}},\,w:\textup{{Ob}},\,f:\textup{{Hom}}(x,y),g:\textup{{Hom}}(y,z),h:\textup{{Hom}}(z,w)}{(h\circ g)\circ f\equiv h\circ(g\circ f)}

A.2
Substitution property

Let
T
be a generalized
\kappa
-algebraic theory. Recall that given
\Delta
,
\{t_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}
expressions and
\{x_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}
variables, then the new expression
\Delta[e_{\alpha}|x_{\alpha}]_{\alpha<\lambda}
denotes the substitution of variables by the expressions.

Definition A.8
.

Let
\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash\Delta
be a derived judgment of
T
. We say that this judgment has the
substitution property
if for every
\vdash\Gamma\,\textup{{Ctxt}}
and expressions
\{t_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}
, such that for all
\alpha<\lambda

\Gamma,\,\{t_{\beta}:\Delta_{\beta}[t_{\gamma}|x_{\gamma}]_{\gamma<\beta}\}_{\beta<\alpha}\vdash t_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}[t_{\beta}|x_{\beta}]_{\beta<\alpha}

are derived rules, then

\Gamma\vdash\Delta[t_{\alpha}|x_{\alpha}]_{\alpha<\lambda}

is a derived rule of
T
.

In
[
Car78
]
it is proven that all derived judgment of a generalized algebraic theory satisfy the substitution property. This is done through a series of results that can be generalized to our setting. The proofs are omitted since they are the same as in the original reference.

Lemma A.9
.

If
\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash\Delta
is a derived judgment of
T
, then the variables that appear in
\Delta
is a subset of
\{x_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}

Proof.

See
[
Car78
, Lemma 1, Section 1.7]
.
∎

Lemma A.10
.

1.

The premise of a derived judgment is a context.

2.

If
\vdash\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\textup{{Ctxt}}
then for
\alpha<\lambda
, we have

\{x_{\beta}:\Delta_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\alpha}\vdash\Delta_{\alpha}\,\textup{{Type}}

Proof.

See
[
Car78
, Lemma 2, Section 1.7]
.
∎

Theorem A.11
.

Every derived judgment of a generalized
\kappa
-algebraic theory has the substitution property.

Proof.

The same proof as in
[
Car78
, 1.7]
applies. This goes by proving that each judgment has the substitution property. For the last two judgments in
definition

A.1
, this is a consequence of rules (
11
) and (
12
) in
definition

A.4
. While for the first two it is done by induction on the derivations. It is shown that each derivation rule of
definition

A.4
preserve the substitution property.
∎

This result has similar consequences of those in
[
Car78
]
. The proofs are analogous or the same. For us, it is only relevant to know that our generalized
\kappa
-algebraic theories are well-defined. That is:

Proposition A.12
.

The derived judgments of a generalized
\kappa
-algebraic theory are well-formed.

Proof.

Again, by induction on the derivations
[
Car78
, pp. 1.33]
.
∎

Both the statement and proof of the next lemma are the same as The Derivation Lemma
[
Car78
, pp. 1.34]
. The proof does not rely on the context size.

Lemma A.13
.

1.

Every derived type judgment of
T
is of the form

\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}\vdash A(t_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}

for some type symbol
A
with introductory rule

\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash A(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}\,\textup{{Type}}

and
\{t_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}
are expressions such that for all
\alpha<\lambda
the rule

\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}\vdash t_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}[t_{\delta}\mid x_{\delta}]_{\delta<\alpha}.

2.

Every term element judgment of
T
is of the form

\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}\vdash x_{\beta}:\Omega

for some
x_{\beta}
and such that
\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}\vdash\Omega_{\beta}\equiv\Omega
, or is of the form

\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}\vdash f(t_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}:\Omega

for some operator symbol
f
of
T
with introductory judgment of the form

\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash f(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}:\Delta

such that for each
\alpha<\lambda
the rules

\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}\vdash t_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}[t_{\delta}\mid x_{\delta}]_{\delta<\alpha}

and

\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}\vdash\Delta[t_{\alpha}\mid x_{\alpha}]_{\alpha<\lambda}\equiv\Omega

are derived rules of
T
.

Proof.

This follows from
definition

A.4
(
10
) and (
11
).
∎

A.3
Equivalence relation on judgments

Throughout this section we work in a generalized
\kappa
-algebraic theory. We first introduce a relation that allows us to identify contexts which express the same meaning, but differ on the variables that are used in them
[
Car78
, 1.13]
.

There is a relation defined on the judgments of the generalized
\kappa
-algebraic theory
T
.

Definition A.14
.

Let
\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash\Delta_{\lambda}\,\textup{{Type}}
and
\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}\vdash\Omega_{\mu}\,\textup{{Type}}
be two type judgments of
T
. We say that

\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash\Delta_{\lambda}\,\textup{{Type}}\approx\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}\vdash\Omega_{\mu}\,\textup{{Type}}

if either:

1.

Both ordinals are successor such that
\lambda=\mu=\nu+1
and for all
\alpha\leq\nu
we have

\{x_{\delta}:\Delta_{\delta}\}_{\delta<\alpha}\vdash\Delta_{\alpha}\equiv\Omega_{\alpha}

is a derived rule of
T
.

2.

Both ordinals are limit ordinals with
\lambda=\mu
and for any successor ordinal
\nu+1<\lambda
we have

\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\nu}\vdash\Delta_{\nu}\,\textup{{Type}}\approx\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\nu}\vdash\Omega_{\nu}\,\textup{{Type}}.

Lemma A.15
.

The relation
\approx
is an equivalence relation on type judgments of the theory
T
.

Proof.

This is an immediate result since we have assumed canonical names for variables. Otherwise, we could repeat the argument as in
[
Car78
, 1.13]
.

∎

Definition A.16
.

Let
\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}
and
\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}
be two contexts. We say that

\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\approx\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}

if and only if
\lambda=\mu
and for all
\alpha<\lambda

\{x_{\delta}:\Delta_{\delta}\}_{\delta<\alpha}\vdash\Delta_{\alpha}\,\textup{{Type}}\approx\{x_{\gamma}:\Omega_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma<\alpha}\vdash\Omega_{\alpha}\,\textup{{Type}}

It follows that this induces an equivalence relation on contexts.

Definition A.17
.

We say that

\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash t:\Delta\approx\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}\vdash s:\Omega

if and only if
\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash\Delta\,\textup{{Type}}\approx\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}\vdash\Omega\,\textup{{Type}}
and
\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash t\equiv s
.

Remark A.18
.

Let
\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}
and
\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}
be two contexts. Assume further that

\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\approx\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}.

Then for all derived rules

\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}\vdash\Omega,

the rule

\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash\Omega

is also a derived rule.

Regardless of its simplicity, this remark is useful in the next:

Corollary A.19
.

The relation
\approx
is an equivalence relation on judgments of the form
\{x_{\beta}:\Delta_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}\vdash t:\Delta.

Proof.

Reflexivity is a consequence of
2
from
definition

A.4
. Assume that
\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash t:\Delta\approx\{x_{\alpha}:\Omega_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash s:\Omega
. Hence, the contexts satisfy
\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\approx\{x_{\alpha}:\Omega_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}
. Applying the symmetry of the relation
\approx
to contexts, and using
remark

A.18
, we see that
\{x_{\alpha}:\Omega_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash t\equiv s
. Then we must have
\{x_{\alpha}:\Omega_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash s:\Delta
and
\{x_{\alpha}:\Omega_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash\Omega\equiv\Delta
. We can apply
4
from
definition

A.4
to conclude that
\{x_{\alpha}:\Omega_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash s\equiv t
, thus proving symmetry. Transitivity is a straightforward application of
remark

A.18
.
∎

Definition A.20
.

A
morphism
between contexts

\langle t_{\beta}\rangle_{\beta<\mu}:\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\to\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}

is
\mu
-sequence of terms
\{t_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}
such that for all
\beta<\mu
we have

\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\vdash t_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}[t_{\gamma}|x_{\gamma}]_{\gamma<\beta}.

Just as in the finite case, with the substitution as composition and the obvious identity, it can be shown that contexts form a category with morphisms as defined above. This is called the
category of realizations
of the theory
T
.
The composition of

\langle t_{\beta}\rangle_{\beta<\mu}:\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\to\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}

and

\langle s_{\delta}\rangle_{\delta<\nu}:\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}\to\{x_{\delta}:\Omega_{\delta}^{\prime}\}_{\delta<\nu}

is the map

\langle s_{\delta}\rangle_{\delta<\nu}\circ\langle t_{\beta}\rangle_{\beta<\mu}:\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\to\{x_{\delta}:\Omega_{\delta}^{\prime}\}_{\delta<\nu}

defined as the sequence
\langle s_{\delta}[\langle t_{\beta}|x_{\beta}\rangle_{\beta<\mu}]\rangle_{\delta<\nu}.

Using the previous relation
\approx
on contexts and rules we induce one on morphisms between contexts.
If we have morphisms

\langle t_{\beta}\rangle_{\beta<\mu}:\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\to\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}\text{ and }\langle t_{\beta}^{\prime}\rangle_{\beta<\mu}:\{x_{\alpha}:\Delta_{\alpha}^{\prime}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}\to\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}^{\prime}\}_{\beta<\mu}

Then

\langle t_{\beta}\rangle_{\beta<\mu}\approx\langle t_{\beta}^{\prime}\rangle_{\beta<\mu}

if and only if

\{x_{\beta}:\Omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta<\mu}\approx\{x_{\beta}^{\prime}:\Omega_{\beta}^{\prime}\}_{\beta<\mu}

and for all
\gamma<\mu
